## 1. Any Unitary Gate Can Be Certified Device-Independently In A Quantum Network

- **Score**: 9.2 / 10  |  **Recommended**: ✅ Yes
- **Source**: Phys. Rev. Lett.  |  **Feed**: Physical Review Letters
- **Direction**: Quantum Information
- **Published**: 2026-07-15
- **Authors**: Shubhayan Sarkar
- **Link**: [http://link.aps.org/doi/10.1103/m1tx-9mx1](http://link.aps.org/doi/10.1103/m1tx-9mx1)
- **Tags**: `device-independent certification`  `self-testing`  `unitary gate`  `quantum network`  `Bell nonlocality`

### TL;DR

This paper proves that any unitary gate can be certified device-independently in a quantum network using a single Bell test.

### Motivation

Device-independent certification of quantum gates is crucial for secure quantum networks, but previous self-testing results were limited to specific gates. A general method was needed.

### Method

The author constructs a Bell-type scenario involving multiple parties and uses the observed correlations to certify the implementation of an arbitrary unitary gate via a robust self-testing protocol.

### Result

It is shown that any unitary gate can be uniquely certified up to local isometries, with robustness bounds provided.

### Conclusion

This work provides a universal framework for DI certification of quantum gates, advancing quantum network verification and cryptography.

*Keywords: device-independent certification, self-testing, unitary gate, quantum network, Bell nonlocality*

## 2. Anomalous Heat Flows And Quantum Otto Engine With (In)definite Causal Order

- **Score**: 9.2 / 10  |  **Recommended**: ✅ Yes
- **Source**: Phys. Rev. Lett.  |  **Feed**: Physical Review Letters
- **Direction**: Information Thermodynamics
- **Published**: 2026-07-14
- **Authors**: Qing-Feng Xue, Qi Zhang, Xu-Cai Zhuang, Ying-Jie Zhang, Yun-Jie Xia, Enrico Russo, Giulio Chiribella, Rosario Lo Franco, And Zhong-Xiao Man
- **Link**: [http://link.aps.org/doi/10.1103/sx1m-pdhz](http://link.aps.org/doi/10.1103/sx1m-pdhz)
- **Tags**: `ai`  `order`  `indefinite causal order`  `quantum Otto engine`  `heat flow`

### TL;DR

This paper demonstrates that indefinite causal order can reverse the natural direction of heat flow and enhance the performance of a quantum Otto engine.

### Motivation

Classical thermodynamics dictates heat flows from hot to cold; the authors explore whether indefinite causal order in quantum processes can violate this principle and improve engine efficiency.

### Method

They consider a quantum Otto engine where the two thermalization strokes are arranged in an indefinite causal order using a quantum switch, and analyze the resulting heat flows and work output.

### Result

Indefinite causal order can cause anomalous heat flow from cold to hot and increase the engine's efficiency beyond the standard Otto cycle limit.

### Conclusion

Indefinite causal order provides a new resource for quantum thermodynamics, enabling counterintuitive heat flows and enhanced performance in thermal machines.

*Keywords: indefinite causal order, quantum Otto engine, heat flow, quantum thermodynamics, quantum switch*

### TL;DR

### Motivation

Conventional quantum computation relies on external classical control, obscuring the true thermodynamic and computational resource costs. A fully self-contained description is needed to understand fundamental limits.

### Method

The authors develop a framework for an autonomous Quantum Processing Unit (aQPU) that integrates an internal quantum clock, instruction register, and memory, treating computation as a thermal machine process.

### Result

The aQPU framework allows investigation of relationships between thermodynamic cost, complexity, speed, and fidelity of quantum computations, revealing trade-offs and physical constraints.

### Conclusion

Modeling quantum computation as an autonomous thermal machine provides a powerful tool to analyze fundamental resource requirements and limitations, advancing the understanding of quantum thermodynamics.

### TL;DR

### Motivation

Spin qubits in phononic quantum devices suffer from short coherence times, limiting their practical use. The authors aim to overcome this by employing continuous acoustic driving to protect spin coherence.

### Method

The experiment uses a diamond spin qubit coupled to a mechanical resonator. Continuous acoustic waves are applied to dynamically decouple the spin from environmental noise, while also enabling fast coherent control via mechanical driving.

### Result

### Conclusion

All-mechanical coherence protection offers a promising route for robust spin qubits in hybrid quantum systems, combining long coherence with fast control.

- **Link**: [https://www.nature.com/articles/s41567-026-03315-2](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41567-026-03315-2)
- **Tags**: `physics`  `quantum error correction`  `trapped ion`  `logical qubit`  `single-ion encoding`

### TL;DR

This paper demonstrates error correction of a logical qubit encoded within the multiple internal states of a single trapped ion, reducing errors and extending coherence time.

### Motivation

Standard quantum error correction requires many physical qubits per logical qubit, which is resource-intensive. Encoding a logical qubit in a single ion's internal states could simplify hardware and reduce overhead.

### Method

The authors encode a logical qubit in the hyperfine and Zeeman sublevels of a single trapped ion, implement error-detecting and error-correcting codes using microwave and laser pulses, and measure error syndromes and correction performance.

### Result

The encoded logical qubit shows reduced error rates and extended coherence time compared to the best physical qubit in the same ion, demonstrating effective error suppression.

### Conclusion

Encoding a logical qubit in a single ion is a viable approach to quantum error correction, potentially reducing the physical qubit overhead for fault-tolerant quantum computing.

*Keywords: quantum error correction, trapped ion, logical qubit, single-ion encoding, coherence time*

### TL;DR

This paper proposes an adiabatic protocol to prepare thermal states on noisy quantum computers and derives an entropy-noise relation.

### Motivation

Preparing thermal states is crucial for quantum thermodynamics and many-body physics, but existing methods are often inefficient or require error correction. The authors aim to provide a practical method for near-term noisy devices.

### Method

The authors design an adiabatic evolution that slowly changes the Hamiltonian from an easy-to-prepare initial state to the target thermal state, and analyze the effect of noise using a master equation. They derive a relation between the entropy of the final state and the noise strength.

### Result

They show that the protocol can prepare thermal states with high fidelity under realistic noise levels, and the entropy-noise relation provides a bound on the achievable purity.

### Conclusion

The work offers a practical route to thermal state preparation on noisy quantum computers, with implications for quantum thermodynamics and simulation of condensed matter systems.

### TL;DR

Introduces a simplified quantum dynamical entropy that grows linearly under continuous monitoring, with a Planckian bound on the growth rate.

### Motivation

To quantify information gain from continuous monitoring in quantum systems and connect entropy growth to fundamental timescales.

### Method

Simplifies the Connes-Narnhofer-Thirring entropy for quantum systems and applies it to monitored observables, deriving a bound from the energy variance.

### Result

The entropy growth rate is bounded by the Planckian time τ_P = ħ/(k_B T), linking quantum dynamics to thermodynamic limits.

### Conclusion

The Planckian bound provides a universal speed limit on information extraction from quantum systems, relevant to quantum thermodynamics and information theory.

*Keywords: quantum dynamical entropy, Planckian bound, continuous monitoring, quantum thermodynamics, information gain*

### TL;DR

Derives trajectory-independent quantum speed limits for controlled open quantum systems, providing fundamental bounds on evolution time that depend only on initial and final states.

### Motivation

Existing quantum speed limits for open systems depend on the control trajectory, limiting their predictive power. The authors aim to find bounds that are independent of the path taken.

### Method

Using geometric and information-theoretic arguments, they derive speed limits based on the Bures angle and the quantum Fisher information, applicable to general Markovian and non-Markovian dynamics.

### Result

### Conclusion

### TL;DR

This paper investigates the quantum Mpemba effect in the relaxation dynamics of operators in open quantum systems, showing that initial states farther from steady state can relax faster.

### Motivation

The quantum Mpemba effect is a counterintuitive phenomenon where a system far from equilibrium relaxes faster than one closer to equilibrium. Understanding this effect in open quantum systems can provide insights into non-equilibrium quantum thermodynamics.

### Method

The authors study the relaxation of operators in open quantum systems using a Lindblad master equation approach, analyzing the time evolution of operator expectation values and comparing relaxation rates for different initial conditions.

### Result

They demonstrate the existence of the Mpemba-like effect for operators, where certain initial operator states that are farther from the steady state exhibit faster relaxation than those initially closer.

### Conclusion

The work extends the concept of the Mpemba effect to operator dynamics in open quantum systems, contributing to the understanding of non-equilibrium relaxation processes and potentially impacting quantum thermodynamics and control.

- **Tags**: `spin`  `quantum thermodynamics`  `strong coupling`  `quench`  `work`

### TL;DR

This paper examines three definitions of internal energy, work, and heat for strongly coupled quantum systems under sudden quenches, showing that only two sets obey the second law.

### Motivation

Strongly coupled quantum systems lack a universally accepted definition of thermodynamic quantities like work and heat, which is crucial for understanding quantum thermodynamics.

### Method

The authors analyze three sets of definitions for internal energy, work, and heat in the context of sudden quenches, and test them against the first and second laws of thermodynamics using a two-spin model.

### Result

All three definitions satisfy the first law by construction, but only two of them obey the second law, indicating that not all definitions are thermodynamically consistent.

### Conclusion

The study provides guidance on choosing appropriate thermodynamic definitions for strongly coupled quantum systems, advancing the field of quantum thermodynamics.

*Keywords: quantum thermodynamics, strong coupling, quench, work, heat*

### Motivation

Standard device-dependent QKD requires fully characterized devices, while fully DI-QKD is impractical due to low key rates. The authors aim to bridge this gap by relaxing trust assumptions only for certain network nodes.

### Method

The protocol achieves significantly higher key rates than fully DI-QKD in asymmetric star networks, with security against general attacks.

### Conclusion

Partially device-independent QKD offers a practical trade-off between security and performance, suitable for realistic quantum network deployments.

- **Tags**: `ai`  `work`  `application`  `fault-tolerance`  `CSS codes`

### TL;DR

This paper proves impossibility results for addressing logical qubits in high-rate CSS codes using local Clifford circuits, and shows that permuting logical qubits via physical permutations is limited by code rate.

### Motivation

Understanding how to implement logical gates on subsets of logical qubits is crucial for fault-tolerant quantum computation, especially with asymptotically good codes.

### Method

The authors use automorphism theory of CSS codes and combinatorial arguments to derive no-go theorems for addressability under local Clifford circuits and physical permutations.

### Result

For CSS codes with non-zero rate, one cannot address logical X, Z, H, or S gates to a strict subset of logical qubits using 1-local Clifford circuits. Also, permuting logical qubits via physical permutations is impossible if the rate > 1/3 and distance ≥ 3.

### Conclusion

There is a fundamental trade-off between addressability and code efficiency, which must be considered for practical quantum computation.

*Keywords: CSS codes, addressability, logical gates, fault-tolerant quantum computation, automorphisms*

### Motivation

Anticoncentration is a key property for quantum advantage and random circuit sampling, but its behavior under noise is not well understood. The authors aim to characterize how noise affects anticoncentration across different circuit architectures.

### Method

The authors study random quantum circuits with local noise using analytical techniques, including mapping to statistical mechanics models and replica methods, to derive universal scaling laws for anticoncentration as a function of circuit depth and noise rate.

## 171. Query And Depth Upper Bounds For Quantum Unitaries Via Grover Search

- **Direction**: Quantum Information
- **Published**: 2026-06-30
- **Authors**: Gregory Rosenthal
- **Link**: [https://quantum-journal.org/papers/q-2026-06-30-2144/](https://quantum-journal.org/papers/q-2026-06-30-2144/)
- **Tags**: `ai`  `Grover search`  `quantum circuit depth`  `unitary synthesis`  `query complexity`

### TL;DR

This paper proves upper bounds on query complexity and circuit depth for implementing arbitrary quantum unitaries using Grover search, with matching lower bounds for certain implementations.

### Motivation

Understanding the resources required to implement arbitrary quantum operations is fundamental to quantum computing. The paper aims to establish tight bounds on query complexity and circuit depth for unitary synthesis.

### Method

The authors reduce unitary implementation to Grover search, using amplitude amplification techniques. For exact implementation, they also construct arbitrary quantum states with linear depth, which can be improved to constant depth with additional gates.

### Result

Any n-qubit unitary can be implemented approximately in time O(2^{n/2}) with query access to a classical oracle, and exactly by a circuit of depth O(2^{n/2}) with one- and two-qubit gates and O(2^n) ancillae. A matching Ω(2^{n/2}) lower bound is proved for a class of implementations.

### Conclusion

The results provide nearly optimal resource bounds for unitary synthesis via Grover search, highlighting the power of quantum search in circuit design.

*Keywords: Grover search, quantum circuit depth, unitary synthesis, query complexity, quantum state preparation*

Understanding the complexity of preparing graph states is important for quantum computing and networking, but the paper focuses on circuit complexity rather than information-theoretic or thermodynamic aspects.

### Method

The authors define CZ-complexity and characterize graph-state transformations using vertex deletions, local complementations, and elementary edge-complementations. They relate CZ-complexity to rank-width and provide algorithms for interval and circle graphs.

### Result

For any graph with n vertices and rank-width r, the CZ-complexity is O(r n) and at least Ω(r) for connected graphs. Interval and circle graphs have O(n) and O(n log n) CZ-complexity, respectively.

### TL;DR

This paper generalizes the MPC-in-the-head paradigm to the quantum setting to build zero-knowledge protocols secure against superposition attacks, with applications to NP and QMA.

### Motivation

Existing zero-knowledge protocols are not secure against quantum verifiers that can obtain superpositions of transcripts, and previous constructions relied on commitments not known from standard assumptions.

### Method

The authors present a quantum generalization of the MPC-in-the-head technique, where the MPC runs a quantum computation, and use it to construct three-round zero-knowledge arguments for NP and QMA based on the LWE assumption.

### Result

Two new three-round zero-knowledge protocols in the common reference string model: one for NP and one for QMA, both secure against superposition attacks and based on standard LWE assumption.

### Conclusion

This work provides the first zero-knowledge protocols for NP and QMA secure against superposition attacks from standard cryptographic assumptions, advancing quantum cryptography.

### Motivation

Understanding why B is preferred in Si and diamond while Al is preferred in SiC is important for optimizing p-type doping in these technologically crucial semiconductors.

### Method

First-principles calculations based on density functional theory (DFT) were performed to compute formation energies and electronic structures of B and Al dopants in Si, diamond, and SiC.

### Result

The preference is explained by differences in atomic size, electronegativity, and lattice relaxation effects, with B being more stable in Si and diamond due to smaller size mismatch, while Al is favored in SiC due to better bond strength and reduced strain.

### Conclusion

The study provides a fundamental understanding of dopant selection in group-IV semiconductors, guiding future doping strategies for electronic devices.

*Keywords: acceptor preference, silicon, diamond, silicon carbide, first-principles calculations*

### TL;DR

This paper shows that the Vicsek model does not exhibit O(2) symmetry breaking, contrary to common belief.

### Motivation

The Vicsek model is a standard model for collective motion, and its phase transition is often attributed to spontaneous breaking of O(2) rotational symmetry. The authors aim to correct this misconception.

### Method

The authors revisit the original Vicsek model, analyze its symmetries, and perform numerical simulations to demonstrate the absence of O(2) symmetry breaking.

### Result

They find that the model's order parameter does not correspond to a broken continuous symmetry, and the phase transition is not associated with O(2) symmetry breaking.

### Conclusion

The widely held belief about O(2) symmetry breaking in the Vicsek model is incorrect; the model's phase transition has a different origin.

### TL;DR

This paper argues that observed behaviors in overdoped cuprates can be explained by substitutional disorder in a d-wave superconductor, rather than requiring non-Fermi-liquid physics.

### Motivation

To resolve the apparent inconsistency between experimental observations in overdoped cuprates and weak-coupling Fermi-liquid-based BCS theory.

### Method

The authors present a perspective based on theoretical arguments and analysis of substitutional disorder effects in a short-correlation-length d-wave superconductor.

### Result

They propose that in the disorder-free limit, a weak-coupling Fermi-liquid description is possible, reconciling theory with experiment.

### Conclusion

Substitutional disorder is key to understanding overdoped cuprate behavior, and disorder-free samples would exhibit conventional Fermi-liquid and BCS physics.

### Motivation

To understand the physical mechanisms that determine whether an earthquake rupture self-arrests or becomes a runaway event, and to explain the Gutenberg-Richter law and dragon-king events.

### Method

The authors develop a dissipation-based framework that separates the onset of unstable slip from conditions for self-sustained rupture propagation, using theoretical modeling and analysis.

### Result

The framework successfully explains the coexistence of self-arresting and runaway earthquakes, and reproduces key statistical features such as the Gutenberg-Richter law and dragon-king events.

### Conclusion

The dissipation-based approach provides a unified understanding of earthquake rupture dynamics, with implications for hazard assessment and prediction of extreme events.

*Keywords: earthquake rupture, self-arresting earthquakes, runaway earthquakes, Gutenberg-Richter law, dragon-king events*

## 524. Machine-learning-enabled Solvent Engineering For Uniform Quantum Dot Packing In Efficient And Stable Quantum-dot Light-emitting Diodes

- **Score**: 0.0 / 10  |  **Recommended**: ❌ No
- **Source**: Rep. Prog. Phys.  |  **Feed**: Reports on Progress in Physics (ROPP)
- **Direction**: General / Other
- **Published**: 2026-07-13
- **Authors**: Beomsoo Chun, Byong Jae Kim, Hyeongjin Kim, Jeong Han Song, Yeongsik Kim, Jaehoon Lim And Jeonghun Kwak
- **Link**: [https://iopscience.iop.org/article/10.1088/1361-6633/ae8470](https://iopscience.iop.org/article/10.1088/1361-6633/ae8470)
- **Tags**: `ai`  `work`  `scattering`  `quantum-dot`  `optimization`

### TL;DR

This paper uses machine learning to optimize solvent mixtures for uniform quantum dot packing in QLEDs, achieving high efficiency and stability.

### Motivation

Achieving uniformly packed emissive layers is a bottleneck for high-performance quantum-dot light-emitting diodes (QLEDs).

### Method

Five solvent parameters were evaluated, and multiple regression models (including support vector regression) were trained against film uniformity from atomic force microscopy to predict optimal solvent mixtures.

### Result

The optimized mixed solvent yielded superior packing homogeneity, and QLEDs achieved an external quantum efficiency of 20.6% and operational lifetime of 468.5 h at 20,000 cd m−2.

### Conclusion

Packing homogeneity is a decisive factor for device performance, and the data-driven framework is generalizable for solution-processed devices.

*Keywords: quantum dots, machine learning, solvent engineering, QLED, film uniformity*

Most ice-sheet models omit fracture mechanics, potentially underestimating sea-level contributions.

- **Tags**: `synthesis`  `biosynthesis`  `GUN4`  `chlorophyll biosynthesis`  `phytobilin`

### TL;DR

This paper investigates the role of GUN4 protein in chlorophyll biosynthesis, specifically its covalent binding to phytobilins and a potential photoprotective mechanism.

### Motivation

Understanding the regulation of chlorophyll biosynthesis is crucial for photosynthesis; GUN4 is known to bind tetrapyrroles and may protect the magnesium chelatase complex from photooxidative damage.

### Method

The authors use biochemical assays, spectroscopy, and structural analysis to characterize covalent adducts between GUN4 and phytobilins, and assess their photoprotective function.

### Result

GUN4 forms covalent adducts with phytobilins, which may sequester these photoreactive molecules and prevent damage to the chlorophyll biosynthesis machinery.

### Conclusion

The findings suggest a novel photoprotective role for GUN4 in chlorophyll biosynthesis, expanding its known functions beyond regulation of magnesium chelatase.

*Keywords: GUN4, chlorophyll biosynthesis, phytobilin, photoprotection, tetrapyrrole*

### TL;DR

This paper studies dendritic morphology and synaptic nonlinearities in human cortical neurons using a deep-learning-based Functional Complexity Index.

### Motivation

To understand what makes human cognition distinctive at the single-neuron level.

### Method

Introduced a deep-learning-based Functional Complexity Index (FCI) to quantify input-output complexity of individual neurons.

### Result

FCI reveals that human cortical neurons exhibit higher functional complexity due to dendritic morphology and synaptic nonlinearities.

### Conclusion

Dendritic and synaptic properties enhance computational capabilities of human neurons, potentially underlying cognitive differences.
